This may influence the degree to which a government may be willing to investigate family incidents.[^cite_note-278] In some cultures, individual members of the family are expected to sacrifice almost completely their own interests in favor of the interests of the family as a whole. What is viewed as an undue expression of personal autonomy is condemned as unacceptable. In these cultures the family predominates over the individual, and where this interacts with cultures of honor, individualistic choice that may damage the family reputation in the community may result in extreme punishment, such as honor killings.[^cite_note-hbv-awareness.com-279]

#### Terminology

In Australia, *domestic violence* refers to occurrences of violence in domestic settings between people in intimate relationships.[^cite_note-280] The term can be altered by each state's legislation and can broaden the spectrum of domestic violence, such as in Victoria, where familial relationships and witnessing any type of violence in the family is defined as a *family violence incident*.[^cite_note-281] In the [Nordic countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nordic_countries) the term *violence in close relations* is used in legal and policy contexts.[^cite_note-282]

#### Knowledge of legal rights

Domestic violence occurs in immigrant communities, and often there is little awareness in these communities of the laws and policies of the host country. A study among first-generation South Asians in the UK found that they had little knowledge about what constituted criminal behavior under the English law. The researchers found that "there was certainly no awareness that there could be rape within a marriage".[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] A study in Australia showed that among the immigrant women sampled who were abused by partners and did not report it, 16.7% did not know domestic violence was illegal, while 18.8% did not know that they could get protection.[^cite_note-285]

### Ability to leave

The ability of victims of domestic violence to leave the relationship is crucial for preventing further abuse. In traditional communities, divorced women often feel rejected and ostracized. In order to avoid this stigma, many women prefer to remain in the marriage and endure the abuse.[^cite_note-286]

Discriminatory marriage and divorce laws can also play a role in the proliferation of the practice.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] According to [Rashida Manjoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashida_Manjoo), a UN special rapporteur on violence against women:

> In many countries a woman's access to property hinges on her relationship to a man. When she separates from her husband or when he dies, she risks losing her home, land, household goods and other property. Failure to ensure equal property rights upon separation or divorce discourages women from leaving violent marriages, as women may be forced to choose between violence at home and destitution in the street.[^cite_note-289]

The legal inability to obtain a divorce is also a factor in the proliferation of domestic violence.[^cite_note-290] In some cultures where marriages are arranged between families, a woman who attempts a separation or divorce without the consent of her husband and extended family or relatives may risk being subjected to honor-based violence.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-hbv-awareness.com-279]

The custom of bride price also makes leaving a marriage more difficult: if a wife wants to leave, the husband may demand back the bride price from her family.[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294]

In advanced nations like the UK, Domestic violence victims may have difficulties getting alternative housing which can force them to stay in the abusive relationship.[^cite_note-295]

Many domestic violence victims delay leaving the abuser because they have pets and are afraid of what will happen to the pets if they leave. Safehouses need to be more accepting of pets, and many refuse to accept pets.[^cite_note-296]

#### Immigration policies

In some countries, the [immigration policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_policy) is tied to whether the person desiring citizenship is married to his/her sponsor. This can lead to persons being trapped in violent relations – such persons may risk deportation if they attempt to separate (they may be accused of having entered into a [sham marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sham_marriage)).[^cite_note-297][^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300] Often the women come from cultures where they will suffer disgrace from their families if they abandon their marriage and return home, and so they prefer to stay married, therefore remaining locked in a cycle of abuse.[^cite_note-301]

### COVID-19 pandemic

Main article: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_domestic_violence)

Some studies have found some association between the COVID-19 pandemic and an upsurge in the rate of domestic violence.[^cite_note-302] The coping mechanisms adopted by individuals during the state of isolation has been implicated in the increase around the globe.[^cite_note-303] Some of the implications of this restriction period are financial distress, induced stress, frustration, and a resulting quest for coping mechanisms, which could trigger violence.[^cite_note-304]

In major cities in Nigeria, such as Lagos and Abuja, in India, and in Hubei province in China, there was a recorded increase in the level of intimate partner violence.[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306]

An increase in the prevalence of domestic violence during the restrictions has been reported in many countries including the US, China, and many European countries. In India, a 131% increase in domestic violence in areas that had strict lockdown measures was recorded.[^cite_note-307][^cite_note-308]

## Effects

### Physical



[The Ottawa Women's Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enclave:_The_Ottawa_Women's_Monument), in [Minto Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minto_Park), downtown [Ottawa](22219.md), Ontario, Canada, to the women murdered as a result of domestic violence (dedicated in 1992)

Bruises, broken bones, head injuries, lacerations, and internal bleeding are some of the acute effects of a domestic violence incident that require medical attention and hospitalization.[^cite_note-Jones_1997_43–50-309] Some chronic health conditions that have been linked to victims of domestic violence are [arthritis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthritis), [irritable bowel syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irritable_bowel_syndrome), [chronic pain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronic_pain), [pelvic pain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelvic_pain), [ulcers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulcer), and migraines.[^cite_note-310] Victims who are pregnant during a domestic violence relationship experience greater risk of miscarriage, pre-term labor, and injury to or death of the fetus.[^cite_note-Jones_1997_43–50-309]

New research illustrates that there are strong associations between exposure to domestic violence and abuse in all their forms and higher rates of many chronic conditions.[^cite_note-311] The strongest evidence comes from the Adverse Childhood Experiences Study which shows correlations between exposure to abuse or neglect and higher rates in adulthood of chronic conditions, high-risk health behaviors and shortened life span.[^cite_note-312] Evidence of the association between physical health and violence against women has been accumulating since the early 1990s.[^cite_note-313]

### HIV/AIDS

Further information: [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS) and [Women and HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_and_HIV/AIDS)

&#x20;A map of the world where most of the land is colored green or yellow except for sub Saharan Africa which is colored red

Estimated [prevalence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prevalence) in % of HIV among young adults (15–49) per country as of 2008:

[^cite_note-314]

| No data   <0.10   0.10–0.5   0.5–1 | 1–5   5–15   15–50 |
| --- | --- |

The WHO has stated that women in abusive relations are at significantly higher risk of HIV/AIDS. The WHO states that women in violent relationships have difficulty negotiating safer sex with their partners, are often forced to have sex, and find it difficult to ask for appropriate testing when they think they may be infected with H.I.V.[^cite_note-autogenerated3-315] A decade of cross-sectional research from Rwanda, Tanzania, South Africa, and India, has consistently found women who have experienced partner violence to be more likely to be infected with HIV.[^cite_note-UNfacts2014-316] The W\.H.O. stated that:[^cite_note-autogenerated3-315]

> There is a compelling case to end intimate partner violence both in its own right as well as to reduce women and girls' vulnerability to HIV/AIDS. The evidence on the linkages between violence against women and HIV/AIDS highlights that there are direct and indirect mechanisms by which the two interact.

Homosexual romantic relationships are similarly affected by the HIV/AIDS status in domestic violence. Research by Heintz and Melendez found that people in same-sex romantic relationships may have difficulty breaching the topic of safe sex for reasons such as "decreased perception of control over sex, fear of violence, and unequal power distributions..."[^cite_note-Heintz_2006-317] Of those who reported violence in the study, about 50% reported forced sexual experiences, of which only half reported the use of safe-sex measures. Barriers to safer sex included fear of abuse, and deception in safe-sex practices. Heintz and Melendez's research ultimately concluded that sexual assault/abuse in same-sex romantic relationships provides a major concern for HIV/AIDS infection as it decreases instances of safe sex. Furthermore, these incidents create additional fear and stigma surrounding safe-sex conversations and knowing one's STD status.[^cite_note-Heintz_2006-317]

### Psychological

Among victims who are still living with their perpetrators high amounts of stress, fear and anxiety are commonly reported. [Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Depression_\(mood\)) is also common, as victims are made to feel [guilty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guilt_\(emotion\)) for 'provoking' the abuse and are frequently subjected to intense [criticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism). It is reported that 60% of victims meet the [diagnostic criteria for depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rating_scales_for_depression), either during or after termination of the relationship, and have a greatly increased risk of suicide. Those who are battered either emotionally or physically often are also depressed because of a feeling of worthlessness. These feelings often persist long-term and it is suggested that many receive therapy for it because of the heightened risk of suicide and other traumatic symptoms.[^cite_note-318]

In addition to depression, victims of domestic violence also commonly experience long-term anxiety and [panic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic), and are likely to meet the diagnostic criteria for [generalized anxiety disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generalized_anxiety_disorder) and [panic disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_disorder). The most commonly referenced psychological effect of domestic violence is P.T.S.D., which is characterized by [flash-backs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flashback_\(psychology\)), intrusive images, an exaggerated [startle response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Startle_response), [nightmares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nightmare), and avoidance of triggers that are associated with the abuse.[^cite_note-319] Studies have indicated that it is important to consider the effect of domestic violence and its psychophysiologic sequelae on women who are mothers of young children. Several studies have shown that maternal interpersonal violence-related P.T.S.D. can, despite a traumatized mother's best efforts, interfere with her child's response to the domestic violence and other traumatic events.[^cite_note-care-16][^cite_note-320]

### Financial

A 2024 study in the Quarterly Journal of Economics, which used Finnish administrative data with unique identifiers for perpetrators and victims of domestic violence, found that "women who begin relationships with (eventually) physically abusive men suffer large and significant earnings and employment falls immediately upon cohabiting with the abusive partner."[^cite_note-\:16-321] This contributes to economic dependence on the abuser that makes it hard for the victim to exit the relationship.[^cite_note-\:16-321]

Due to economic abuse and isolation, many victims have little money of their own and few people on whom they can rely when seeking help. This has been shown to be one of the greatest obstacles facing victims of domestic violence, and the strongest factor that can discourage them from leaving their perpetrators.[^cite_note-Stop_Violence_Against_Women-322]

In addition to lacking financial resources, victims of domestic violence often lack specialized skills, education, and training that are necessary to find gainful employment, and also may have several children to support. In 2003, thirty-six major US cities cited domestic violence as one of the primary causes of homelessness in their areas.[^cite_note-aclu.org-323] It has also been reported that one out of every three women are homeless due to having left a domestic violence relationship. If a victim is able to secure rental housing, it is likely that her apartment complex will have [zero tolerance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero_tolerance) policies for crime; these policies can cause them to face eviction even if they are the victim (not the perpetrator) of violence.[^cite_note-aclu.org-323] While the number of [women's shelters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_shelters) and community resources available to domestic violence victims has grown tremendously, these agencies often have few employees and hundreds of victims seeking assistance which causes many victims to remain without the assistance they need.[^cite_note-Stop_Violence_Against_Women-322]

Women and children experiencing domestic violence undergo [occupational apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupational_apartheid); they are typically denied access to desired occupations.[^cite_note-\:0-324] Abusive partners may limit occupations and create an occupationally void environment which reinforces feelings of low self-worth and poor self-efficacy in their ability to satisfactorily perform everyday tasks.[^cite_note-\:0-324] In addition, work is impacted by functional losses, an inability to maintain necessary employment skills, and an inability to function within the work place. Often, the victims are very isolated from other relationships as well such as having few to no friends, this is another method of control for the abuser.[^cite_note-\:3-325]

### On children



Children in [Solomon Islands](265083.md) watch a play on gender-based violence.

Main articles: [Effects of domestic violence on children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_domestic_violence_on_children) and [Child abuse § Effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_abuse#Effects)

There has been an increase in acknowledgment that a child who is exposed to domestic abuse during their upbringing will suffer developmental and psychological damage.[^cite_note-Dodd2009-326] During the mid-1990s, the [Adverse Childhood Experiences Study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adverse_Childhood_Experiences_Study) found that children who were exposed to domestic violence and other forms of abuse had a higher risk of developing mental and physical health problems.[^cite_note-327] Because of the awareness of domestic violence that some children have to face, it also generally impacts how the child develops emotionally, socially, behaviorally and cognitively.[^cite_note-328]

Some emotional and behavioral problems that can result due to domestic violence include increased aggressiveness, anxiety, and changes in how a child socializes with friends, family, and authorities.[^cite_note-Dodd2009-326] Depression, emotional insecurity, and mental health disorders can follow due to traumatic experiences.[^cite_note-329] Problems with attitude and cognition in schools can start developing, along with a lack of skills such as problem-solving.[^cite_note-Dodd2009-326] Correlation has been found between the experience of abuse and neglect in childhood and perpetrating domestic violence and sexual abuse in adulthood.[^cite_note-Sadeler-330]

Additionally, in some cases the abuser will purposely abuse the mother or father[^cite_note-Damant-331] in front of the child to cause a ripple effect, hurting two victims simultaneously.[^cite_note-Damant-331] Children may intervene when they witness severe violence against a parent, which can place a child at greater risk for injury or death.[^cite_note-332] It has been found that children who witness mother-assault are more likely to exhibit symptoms of P.T.S.D.[^cite_note-Lehmann-333] Consequences to these children are likely to be more severe if their assaulted mother develops P.T.S.D. and does not seek treatment due to her difficulty in assisting her child with processing its own experience of witnessing the domestic violence.[^cite_note-334]

### On responders

See also: [Vicarious traumatization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicarious_traumatization) and [Burnout (psychology)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burnout_\(psychology\))

An analysis in the U.S. showed that 106 of the 771 officer killings between 1996 and 2009 occurred during domestic violence interventions.[^cite_note-officers-335] Of these, 51% were defined as unprovoked or as ambushes, taking place before officers had made contact with suspects. Another 40% occurred after contact and the remainder took place during tactical situations (those involving hostages and attempts to overcome barricades).[^cite_note-officers-335] The [FBI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FBI)'s [LEOKA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Incident-Based_Reporting_System#LEOKA_segment) system grouped officer domestic violence response deaths into the category of disturbances, along with "bar fights, gang matters, and persons brandishing weapons", which may have given rise to a misperception of the risks involved.[^cite_note-officers-335][^cite_note-BuzawaBuzawa2003-336]

Due to the gravity and intensity of hearing victims' stories of abuse, professionals (social workers, police, counselors, therapists, advocates, medical professionals) are at risk themselves for secondary or vicarious trauma, which causes the responder to experience trauma symptoms similar to the original victim after hearing about the victim's experiences with abuse.[^cite_note-Iliffe-337] Research has demonstrated that professionals who experience vicarious trauma show signs of an exaggerated startle response, [hypervigilance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypervigilance), nightmares and [intrusive thoughts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intrusive_thought) although they have not experienced a trauma personally and do not qualify for clinical diagnoses of P.T.S.D.[^cite_note-Iliffe-337]

## Demographics

Further information: [Epidemiology of domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidemiology_of_domestic_violence)

Domestic violence occurs across the world, in various cultures,[^cite_note-watts2002-338] and affects people of all economic statuses;[^cite_note-waits1985-23] however, indicators of lower socioeconomic status (such as unemployment and low income) have been shown to be risk factors for higher levels of domestic violence in several studies.[^cite_note-capaldi2012-339] Worldwide, domestic violence against women is most common in Central Sub-Saharan Africa, Western Sub-Saharan Africa, Andean Latin America, South Asia, Eastern Sub-Saharan Africa, Northern Africa and the Middle East. The lowest prevalence of domestic violence against women is found in Western Europe, East Asia and North America.[^cite_note-340] In diverse countries there are often ethnic and racial differences in victimization and use of services. In the [United States](3434750.md), [white women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_American) and [black women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American) were more likely to be victims of domestic violence assault than were [Asian-American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Americans) or [Hispanic women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_Americans), according to a 2012 study.[^cite_note-341] Non-Hispanic [white women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans) are twice as likely to use domestic violence services as compared with Hispanic women.[^cite_note-342] In the United Kingdom there is also much research to suggest that income is closely associated with domestic violence, as domestic violence is consistently more common in families with low income.[^cite_note-343] The [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md) reports that in the United States, 41% of women and 26% of men experience domestic violence within their lifetime.[^cite_note-344]

In the United Kingdom, statistics show that 1 in 3 victims of domestic abuse are male. This figure comes from the office of National Statistics, which also states that 1 in 7 men and 1 in 4 women will be a victim at some point in their lifetime.[^cite_note-345]

A significant proportion of domestic violence is committed by [repeat offenders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recidivism), which were previously released by the [criminal justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_justice) system despite posing a risk to the [public safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_security).[^cite_note-x590-346]

| [Venezuela](32374.md) | 0.4 | 2018 |

### Underreporting

Domestic violence is among the most underreported crimes worldwide for both men and women.[^cite_note-Cengage_Learning-11][^cite_note-concannon-12] A 2011 review article by intimate partner violence researcher Ko Ling Chan found men tended to underreport their own perpetration of domestic violence while women were more likely to underreport their victimization and overestimate their own violence perpetration.[^cite_note-chan2011-348] Financial or familial dependence, normalization of violence, and [self-blaming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blame) were found to reduce the likelihood of self-reporting victimization in women. By contrast, fear and avoidance of legal consequences, the tendency to blame their partner, and a narrative focus on their own needs and emotions reduced the likelihood of self-reporting perpetration in men.[^cite_note-chan2011-348]

A 2014 study conducted across the 28 member states of the [EU](9317.md) found that only 14% of women reported their most serious incident of intimate partner violence to the police.[^cite_note-UNfacts2014-316] A 2009 report on domestic violence in [Northern Ireland](21265.md) found that "under-reporting is a concern and domestic abuse is the least likely of all violent crimes to be reported to the police".[^cite_note-349]

Male victims are less likely to report domestic violence than female victims,[^cite_note-\:52-350][^cite_note-Crime_Council-351][^cite_note-352] and may face additional gender-related barriers in reporting due to [social stigmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stigma) regarding male victimization and an increased likelihood of being overlooked by health-care providers.[^cite_note-Riviello_129-353][^cite_note-Finley_163-354][^cite_note-Criminal_Investigation-355][^cite_note-Clearinghouse-356]

During lockdown for [COVID-19](63030231.md), some victims were inside their homes with their abusers. They were left without an escape during this period, which further led to underreporting.[^cite_note-\:10-357] Activists in China have stated that 90% of domestic violence cases have resulted because of the lockdown. The situation was the same in several European countries struggling with the virus.[^cite_note-\:10-357]

### Gender differences

There continues to be some debate regarding gender differences with relation to domestic violence. Limitations of [methodology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methodology), such as the [conflict tactics scale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conflict_tactics_scale), that fail to capture injury, homicide, and sexual violence rates,[^cite_note-358] context (e.g. motivations, fear),[^cite_note-359] disparate [sampling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampling_\(statistics\)) procedures, respondent reluctance to self-report, and differences in [operationalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operationalization) all pose challenges to existing research.[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-chan2011-348][^cite_note-361][^cite_note-362] [Normalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Normalization_\(sociology\)) of domestic violence in those who experience covert forms of abuse, or have been abused by multiple partners, for long periods of time, reduces the likelihood of recognizing, and therefore reporting, domestic violence.[^cite_note-363] Many organizations have made efforts to use gender-neutral terms when referring to perpetration and victimization. For example, using broader terms like *family violence* rather than *violence against women*.[^cite_note-364]

Findings often indicate that when committing domestic violence, women are more likely than men to be motivated by self-defense and/or retaliation or fear.[^cite_note-Swan-365][^cite_note-366][^cite_note-\:8-367][^cite_note-368] A 2010 systematic review of the literature on women's perpetration of I.P.V. found that the common motives for female-on-male I.P.V. were anger, a need for attention, or as a response to their partners' violence. It also stated that while self-defense and retaliation were common motivations, distinguishing between self-defense and retaliation was difficult.[^cite_note-369] Another review found than men and women commit equal levels of physical or psychological aggression, and men are more likely to commit sexual abuse, coercive control and stalking.[^cite_note-Swan-365] Another Family violence research by [Murray A. Straus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Murray_A._Straus) concluded that most I.P.V. perpetrated by women against men is not motivated by self-defense.[^cite_note-\:8-367][^cite_note-370] Other research supports Straus's conclusion about female-perpetrated I.P.V. but adds that men are more likely to retaliate for being hit.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372] Straus's research was criticized by Loseke et al. for using narrow definitions of self-defense.[^cite_note-373]

Sherry Hamby states that sexual violence is often left out of measures of I.P.V. When sexual violence is accounted for, female perpetrators make up less than 10%.[^cite_note-\:10-357] She says that males' self-reports of victimization are unreliable, as they consistently underreport their own violence perpetration,[^cite_note-\:9-374][^cite_note-\:8-367] and also that both men and women use violence for coercive control.[^cite_note-\:9-374] Coercive control is when one person uses a variety of violent tactics to control and dominate the other, with little empathy; victims often resist with physical violence.[^cite_note-375] It is generally perpetrated by men against women, and is the most likely of the types to cause trauma bonding[^cite_note-376] and require medical services.[^cite_note-Marx-377][^cite_note-Emery-378] A 2011 review by researcher Chan Ko Ling from the [University of Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hong_Kong) found that perpetration of minor partner violence was equal for both men and women but more severe partner violence was far likelier to be perpetrated by men.[^cite_note-chan2011-348] His analysis found that men were more likely to beat up, choke or strangle their partners while women were more likely to throw objects, slap, kick, bite, punch, or hit with an object.[^cite_note-chan2011-348]

Researchers have also found significantly different outcomes for men and women in response to intimate partner violence.[^cite_note-\:12-379] Hamberger's review in 2005 found that men tend to respond to female partner-initiated I.P.V. with laughter and amusement.[^cite_note-380] Researchers report that male violence causes great fear, "fear is the force that provides battering with its power" and "injuries help sustain the fear."[^cite_note-381] A 2013 review examined studies from five [continents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continent) and the correlation between a country's level of gender inequality and rates of domestic violence. The authors found that when partner abuse is defined broadly to include emotional abuse, any kind of hitting, and who hits first, partner abuse is relatively even. They also stated if one examines who is physically harmed and how seriously, expresses more fear, and experiences subsequent psychological problems, domestic violence is significantly gendered toward women as victims.[^cite_note-Eugenio-382]

Laws on domestic violence vary by country. While it is generally outlawed in the [Western world](21208200.md), this is not the case in many [developing countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developing_countries). For instance, in 2010, the United Arab Emirates's Supreme Court ruled that a man has the right to physically discipline his wife and children as long as he does not leave physical marks.[^cite_note-383] The social acceptability of domestic violence also differs by country. While in most developed countries domestic violence is considered unacceptable by most people, in many regions of the world the views are different: according to a [U.N.I.C.E.F.](19698110.md) survey, the percentage of women aged 15–49 who think that a husband is justified in hitting or beating his wife under certain circumstances is, for example: 90% in [Afghanistan](737.md) and [Jordan](7515964.md), 87% in [Mali](19127.md), 86% in [Guinea](12176.md) and [Timor-Leste](19159399.md), 81% in [Laos](17752.md), 80% in [Central African Republic](5478.md).[^cite_note-384] Refusing to submit to a husband's wishes is a common reason given for justification of violence in developing countries:[^cite_note-385] for instance 62.4% of women in [Tajikistan](30108.md) justify wife beating if the wife goes out without telling the husband; 68% if she argues with him; 47.9% if she refuses to have sex with him.[^cite_note-386]

#### Women and girls

See also: [Violence against women § Domestic violence](3634424.md#Domestic_violence)



Campaign against domestic violence in [Uganda](31816.md)

The [UN Population Fund](63952.md) found violence against women and girls to be one of the most prevalent [human rights](13831.md) violations worldwide, stating that "one in three women will experience physical or sexual abuse in her lifetime."[^cite_note-387] Violence against women tends to be less prevalent in developed Western nations, and more normalized in the developing world.[^cite_note-388]

Wife beating was made illegal nationally in the US by 1920.[^cite_note-389][^cite_note-390] Although the exact rates are disputed, there is a large body of cross-cultural evidence that women are subjected to domestic violence significantly more often than men.[^cite_note-Grodin-6][^cite_note-Compton2010-391][^cite_note-Brinkerhoff2008-392] In addition, there is broad consensus that women are more often subjected to severe forms of abuse and are more likely to be injured by an abusive partner, and this is exacerbated by economic or social dependence.[^cite_note-McQuigg-5][^cite_note-europa1-28][^cite_note-Compton2010-391][^cite_note-Brinkerhoff2008-392]

The 1993 UN [Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_on_the_Elimination_of_Violence_against_Women) (DEVAW) states that "[violence against women](3634424.md) is a manifestation of historically unequal power relations between men and women, which has led to domination over and discrimination against women by men and to the prevention of the full advancement of women, and that violence against women is one of the crucial social mechanisms by which women are forced into a subordinate position compared with men".[^cite_note-UN_GA_Res-38][^cite_note-393] The DEVAW classifies violence against women into three categories: that occurring in the family (domestic violence), that occurring within the general community, and that perpetrated or condoned by the State.[^cite_note-UN_GA_Res-38]

The Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment, and Eradication of Violence against Women defines violence against women as "any act or conduct, based on gender, which causes death or physical, sexual or psychological harm or suffering to women, whether in the public or the private sphere".[^cite_note-Treaties-394] Similarly with the DEVAW, it classifies violence against women into three categories, one of which is domestic violence – defined as violence against women which takes place "within the family, domestic unit or within any other interpersonal relationship, whether or not the perpetrator shares or has shared the same residence with the woman".[^cite_note-Treaties-394]

The [Maputo Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maputo_Protocol) of the [African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_Charter_on_Human_and_Peoples'_Rights) adopted a broader definition, defining violence against women as: "all acts perpetrated against women which cause or could cause them physical, sexual, psychological, and economic harm, including the threat to take such acts; or to undertake the imposition of arbitrary restrictions on or deprivation of fundamental freedoms in private or public life in peacetime and during situations of armed conflicts or of war".[^cite_note-395]

The Istanbul Convention states: "'violence against women' is understood as a violation of human rights *and a form of discrimination against women* ..." (Article 3 – Definitions).[^cite_note-conventions.coe.int-140] In the landmark case of *Opuz v Turkey*, the [European Court of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Court_of_Human_Rights) held for the first time that gender-based domestic violence is a form of discrimination under the [European Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Convention_on_Human_Rights).[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-397]

According to one study, the percentage of women who have reported being physically abused by an intimate partner vary from 69% to 10% depending on the country.[^cite_note-398] In the US, it is estimated that intimate partner violence accounts for 15% of all violent crime.[^cite_note-399] Research by the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md) in 2017 found that over half of all female homicides are committed by intimate partners, 98% of whom are men.[^cite_note-\:7-75]

[Femicide](1686164.md) is usually defined as the sex-based killing of women or girls by men, although the exact definitions vary. [Feminist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminism) author [Diana E. H. Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diana_E._H._Russell) first defined the term in 1976 as "the killing of females by males because they are female." Femicides often occur in the context of domestic violence, such as honor killings or dowry killings. For statistical purposes, femicide is often defined as any killing of a woman. The top countries by rate of femicide are [El Salvador](9356.md), [Jamaica](15660.md), [Guatemala](17238567.md), [South Africa](17416221.md) and [Mexico](3966054.md) (data from 2004 to 2009).[^cite_note-smallarmssurvey.org-400] However, in El Salvador and Colombia, which have a very high rate of femicide, only 3% of all femicides are committed by a current or former intimate partner, while in [Cyprus](5593.md), [France](5843419.md), and [Portugal](23033.md) former and current partners are responsible for more than 80% of all cases of femicide.[^cite_note-smallarmssurvey.org-400]

#### Men and boys

Main article: [Domestic violence against men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence_against_men)



[Kalighat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalighat) painting, *Woman Striking Man with Broom*, [Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcutta), [India](14533.md), 1875

Research on men and domestic violence focuses on men as both perpetrators and victims of violence, as well as on how to involve men and boys in anti-violence work.[^cite_note-401] Domestic violence against men includes physical, emotional and sexual forms of abuse, including mutual violence.[^cite_note-402][^cite_note-dap-403] Male victims may be reluctant to get help for various reasons.[^cite_note-kumar-404] One study investigated whether women who assaulted their male partners were more likely to avoid arrest even when the male contacts police, and found that, "police are particularly unlikely to arrest women who assault their male partners." The reason being that they "assume that the man can protect himself from his female partner and that a woman's violence is not dangerous unless she assaults someone other than her partner".[^cite_note-405] Another study concluded there is "some support for qualitative research suggesting that court personnel are responsive to the gendered asymmetry of intimate partner violence, and may view female intimate violence perpetrators more as victims than offenders."[^cite_note-406]

### Age groups

#### Parents

Main article: [Parental abuse by children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parental_abuse_by_children)

Abuse of parents by their children, also known as child-to-parent violence (CPV),[^cite_note-407] is one of the most under-reported and under-researched subject areas in the field of [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology). Parents are quite often subject to levels of [childhood aggression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Childhood_aggression) in excess of normal childhood aggressive outbursts, typically in the form of [verbal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Verbal_abuse) or [physical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_abuse) abuse. Parents feel a sense of [shame](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shame) and [humiliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humiliation) to have that problem, so they rarely seek help.[^cite_note-408][^cite_note-409]

Parental abuse has been defined by Cottrell as "any harmful act of a teenage child intended to gain power and control over a parent. The abuse can be physical, psychological, or financial", and often takes places during the teen years (often from 12 to 17), but it can happen earlier than that. The effects of experiencing abuse from one's child can be profound. In the short term, ongoing parent abuse has been found to impact on a parent's and other family members' physical and psychological health, with specific negative emotions such as fear, shame, guilt and despair commonly reported.[^cite_note-410]

A child may become abusive if they have suffered some form of abuse themselves, although this may not always be the case. Parent abuse may derive not only from individualized issues, but also from structural societal and cultural factors.[^cite_note-411]

#### Adolescents and young adults

Main article: [Teen dating violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teen_dating_violence)

Among adolescents, researchers have primarily focused on heterosexual Caucasian populations.[^cite_note-O'Donohue-412] The literature indicates that rates are similar for the number of girls and boys in heterosexual relationships who report experiencing intimate-partner violence (I.P.V.), or that girls in heterosexual relationships are more likely than their male counterparts to report perpetrating I.P.V.[^cite_note-O'Donohue-412][^cite_note-Mitchell-413][^cite_note-Williams-414] Ely et al. stated that unlike domestic violence in general, equal rates of I.P.V. perpetration is a unique characteristic with regard to adolescent dating violence, and that this is "perhaps because the period of adolescence, a special developmental state, is accompanied by sexual characteristics that are distinctly different from the characteristics of adult."[^cite_note-Rapp-Paglicci-415] Wekerle and Wolfe theorized that "a mutually coercive and violent dynamic may form during adolescence, a time when males and females are more equal on a physical level" and that this "physical equality allows girls to assert more power through physical violence than is possible for an adult female attacked by a fully physically mature man."[^cite_note-Rapp-Paglicci-415] Sherry Hamby stated that horseplay and joking among adolescents and young adults is common and that "a small but growing body of research indicates that females may be more likely to include this sort of joking around in responses to IPV questionnaires than males", leading to an apparent gender parity in some studies.[^cite_note-416]

While the general literature indicates that adolescent boys and girls engage in I.P.V. at about equal rates, females are more likely to use less dangerous forms of physical violence (e.g. pushing, pinching, slapping, scratching or kicking), while males are more likely to punch, strangle, beat, burn, or threaten with weapons. Males are also more likely to use sexual aggression, although both sexes are equally likely to pressure their partners into sexual activities. In addition, females are four times more likely to respond as having experienced rape and are more likely to suffer fatal injuries inflicted by their partners, or to need psychological help as a result of the abuse. Females are more likely to consider I.P.V. a serious problem than are their male counterparts, who are more likely to disregard female-perpetrated I.P.V.[^cite_note-O'Donohue-412][^cite_note-Mitchell-413][^cite_note-Poet-417] Along with form, motivations for violence also vary by gender: females are likely to perpetrate violence in self-defense, while males are likely to perpetrate violence to exert power or control.[^cite_note-O'Donohue-412][^cite_note-Mitchell-413] The self-defense aspect is supported by findings that previous victimization is a stronger predictor of perpetration in females than in males.[^cite_note-Edwards-418] Other research indicates that boys who have been abused in childhood by a family member are more prone to I.P.V. perpetration, while girls who have been abused in childhood by a family member are prone to lack empathy and [self-efficacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-efficacy), but the risks for the likelihood of I.P.V. perpetration and victimization among adolescents vary and are not well understood.[^cite_note-Mitchell-413] Hamby's 2018 literature review of 33 studies, using a scale that rules out the false positives of horseplay and joking, indicates that males report perpetrating significantly more violence than females.[^cite_note-419]

#### Children

Main article: [Child abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_abuse)



&#x20; Countries where parental child [corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_punishment_in_the_home) is outlawed.

&#x20;The views on child corporal punishment vary around the world. In most countries parental corporal punishment is not considered a form of domestic violence (if not excessive), but some countries, mostly in Europe and Latin America, have made any form of child corporal punishment illegal.

[^cite_note-endcorporalpunishment.org-420]

There is a strong link between domestic violence and child abuse. Since domestic violence is a pattern of behavior, these incidences may increase in severity and frequency, resulting in an increased probability the children themselves will become victims. The estimated overlap between domestic violence and child abuse ranges from 30% to 50%.[^cite_note-421]

Today, [corporal punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_punishment) of children by their parents remains legal in a majority of countries, but in Western countries that still allow the practice there are strict limits on what is permitted. The first country to outlaw parental corporal punishment was [Sweden](5058739.md) (parents' right to spank their own children was first removed in 1966[^cite_note-Durrant_1996-422]), and it was explicitly prohibited by law from July 1979. As of 2021, corporal punishment of children is banned in all settings, including by parents, in 63 countries.[^cite_note-423]

### Same-sex relationships

Main article: [Domestic violence in same-sex relationships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence_in_same-sex_relationships)

Further information: [Sexual assault of LGBT persons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_assault_of_LGBT_persons)

Historically, domestic violence has been seen as a heterosexual family issue and little interest has been directed at violence in [same-sex relationships](19771642.md),[^cite_note-Aguinaldo-424] but domestic violence does occur in same-sex relationships as well. The *Encyclopedia of Victimology and Crime Prevention* states, "For several methodological reasons – nonrandom sampling procedures and self-selection factors, among others – it is not possible to assess the extent of same-sex domestic violence. Studies on abuse between gay couples usually rely on small convenience samples such as gay members of an association."[^cite_note-Fisher-425] Timothy Laurie and Hannah Stark write that "There has been a persistent erasure of [LGBTQIA+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQIA+) identities in [heteronormative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heteronormative) framings of intimate partner violence, including the exclusion of sexual diversity from formative texts on coercive control."[^cite_note-426]

A 1999 analysis of nineteen studies of partner abuse concluded that "research suggests that lesbians and gay men are just as likely to abuse their partners as heterosexual men."[^cite_note-Elsevier-427] In 2011, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention released the 2010 results of their National Intimate Partner and Sexual Violence Survey and report that 44% of gay women, 61% of bisexual women, and 35% of heterosexual women have experienced domestic violence.[^cite_note-NISVS-428] This same report states that 26% of homosexual men, 37% of bisexual men, and 29% of heterosexual men have experienced domestic violence.[^cite_note-NISVS-428] A 2013 study showed that 40.4% of self-identified gay women and 56.9% of bisexual women have reported being victims of partner violence.[^cite_note-Chen_2013-429] In 2014, national surveys indicated that anywhere from 25 to 50% of gay and bisexual males have experienced physical violence from a partner.[^cite_note-Finneran_2014-430] Some sources indicate that gay couples experience domestic violence at the same frequency as heterosexual couples,[^cite_note-Karmen-431] while other state that domestic violence among gays and bisexuals might be higher than among heterosexuals, that gays and bisexuals are less likely to report domestic violence that has occurred in their intimate relationships than heterosexual couples are, or that [gay female couples experience domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence_in_lesbian_relationships) less than heterosexual couples do.[^cite_note-Hampton-432] One study focusing on Hispanic men indicated that gay men are less likely to have been perpetrators or victims of domestic violence than heterosexual men but that bisexual men are more likely to have been both.[^cite_note-433] By contrast, some researchers commonly assume that female-female couples experience domestic violence at the same rate as heterosexual couples, and have been more cautious when reporting domestic violence among male-male couples.[^cite_note-Fisher-425]

Homosexual relationships have been identified as a risk factor for abuse in certain populations.[^cite_note-Siemieniuk_2010_763–770-64] Nonheterosexuals in some parts of the world have very little legal protection from domestic violence, because [homosexuality is criminalized](69713049.md) (as of 2014, same-sex sexual acts are punishable by imprisonment in 70 countries and by death in another 5 countries)[^cite_note-434] and these legal prohibitions prevent victims of domestic violence from reporting the abuse to authorities.[^cite_note-435]

People in same-sex romantic relationships face special obstacles in dealing with the issues that some researchers have labeled the *double closet*. A 1997 Canadian study by Mark W. Lehman suggests similarities include frequency (approximately one in every four couples); manifestations (emotional, physical, financial, etc.); coexistent situations (unemployment, substance abuse, low self-esteem); victims' reactions (fear, feelings of helplessness, hypervigilance); and reasons for staying (love, can work it out, things will change, denial).[^cite_note-Lehman_1997-436] Studies conducted by Emory University in 2014 identified 24 triggers for partner violence through web-based surveys, ranging from drugs and alcohol to safe-sex discussions.[^cite_note-Finneran_2014-430] A general theme of power and control seems to underlie abuse in both heterosexual and homosexual relationships.[^cite_note-Chen_2013-429]

At the same time, significant differences, unique issues, and deceptive myths are typically present.[^cite_note-Lehman_1997-436] Lehman, regarding his 1997 survey, points to added discrimination and fears that gay people may face. This includes potential dismissal by police and some social services, a lack of support from peers, fear of attracting stigma toward the [gay community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gay_community), the impact of an [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS) status in keeping romantic partners together (due to health-care insurance/access, or guilt), threat of [outing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outing), and encountering supportive services that are targeted, or structured for the needs of heterosexual women, and may not meet the needs of homosexuals. This service structure can make nonheterosexual victims feel even more isolated and misunderstood than they may already because of their minority status.[^cite_note-437] Lehman, however, stated that "due to the limited number of returned responses and non-random sampling methodology the findings of this work are not generalizable beyond the sample" of 32 initial respondents and final 10 who completed the more in-depth survey.[^cite_note-Lehman_1997-436] Particularly, sexual stressors and an HIV/AIDS status have emerged as significant differences in same-sex partner violence.[^cite_note-Finneran_2014-430]

## Management

Main article: [Management of domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Management_of_domestic_violence)

Management of domestic violence may take place through medical services, law enforcement,[^cite_note-438][^cite_note-439] counseling, and other forms of prevention and intervention. Participants in domestic violence may require medical treatment, such as examination by a [family physician](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_practitioner), other [primary care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_care) provider,[^cite_note-440] or [emergency room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_room) physicians.[^cite_note-441]

Counseling is another means of managing the effects of domestic violence. For the victim of abuse, counseling may include an assessment of the presence,[^cite_note-Lawson_2003_19–33-442] extent and types of abuse.[^cite_note-Lawson_2003_19–33-442] A lethality assessment is a tool that can assist in determining the best course of treatment for a client, as well as helping the client to recognize dangerous behaviors and more subtle abuse in their relationship.[^cite_note-443] In a study of victims of domestic violence-related attempted homicide, only about one-half of the participants recognized that their perpetrator was capable of killing them, as many domestic violence victims minimize the true seriousness of their situation.[^cite_note-444] Another important component is safety planning, which allows the victim to plan for dangerous situations they may encounter, and is effective regardless of their decision on whether they remain with their perpetrator.[^cite_note-azcadv.org-70]

Counseling may be used by offenders to minimize the risk of future domestic violence,[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446] or to stop the violence and repair the harm it has caused.[^cite_note-Augusta-Scott,_T._2017-447] Most commonly, to date, convicted or self-referring offenders undertake programmes for perpetrators of intimate partner violence. These are delivered in a group format, one or two hours per week, over a set time period. Programme facilitators guide participants through a curriculum of adult education-style modules, which draw on a variety of therapeutic approaches, but predominantly cognitive behavioural therapy and psycho-education. A debate on the effectiveness of these programmes is ongoing. While some partners and ex-partners of offenders have experienced improvements in their situation, others have not, and there also appears to be a risk of doing harm.[^cite_note-448] Along with using group work, there are other approaches that incorporate individual and conjoint conversations to help stop the violence and restore the victims' safety and respect.[^cite_note-Augusta-Scott,_T._2017-447]

Prevention and intervention includes ways to prevent domestic violence by offering safe shelter, [crisis intervention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crisis_intervention), advocacy and education and prevention programs. Community screening for domestic violence can be more systematic in health-care settings, emergency departments, behavioral health settings, court systems and in cases of violence against non-humans. Tools are being developed to facilitate domestic violence screening such as mobile applications.[^cite_note-449][^cite_note-450] The [Duluth Model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duluth_Model) or Domestic Abuse Intervention Project is a program developed to reduce domestic violence against women,[^cite_note-451] which is the first multi-disciplinary program designed to address the issue of domestic violence by coordinating the actions of a variety of agencies dealing with domestic conflict.[^cite_note-452]

[Domestic violence hotlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_domestic_violence_hotlines) offer advice, support and referral services to those in abusive relationships.

Specialized [domestic violence courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence_court) have been increasingly used since the 1980s to adjudicate domestic violence cases in the United States.

## Prevention



Poster against domestic violence in [Bolgatanga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bolgatanga), [Ghana](12067.md)

There exist several strategies that are being used to attempt to prevent or reduce domestic violence. It is important to assess the effectiveness of a strategy that is being implemented.[^cite_note-who.int1-453]

The [Welsh Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Welsh_Government)'s campaign to change attitudes about domestic abuse (short TV advertisement)

Reforming the legislation in order to ensure that domestic violence falls under the scope of the law is important. This may imply repealing existing laws which discriminate against women: according to the W\.H.O., "when the law allows husbands to physically discipline wives, implementing a programme to prevent intimate partner violence may have little impact".[^cite_note-who.int1-453] [Marriage laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_law) are also important; "women should also be able to enter freely into a marriage or to leave it, to obtain financial credit, and to own and administer property."[^cite_note-who.int1-453] Abolishing or restricting the offering and receiving of dowry and bride price and scrutinizing the impact of these transactions on the legislative decisions regarding domestic violence is also important. UN Women has stated that the legislation should ensure that "a perpetrator of \[domestic violence], including marital rape, cannot use the fact that he paid bride price as a defence to a \[domestic violence] charge".[^cite_note-454]

Gender norms that promote the inferiority of women may lead to the abuse of women by intimate partners. The W\.H.O. writes that, "Dismantling hierarchical constructions of masculinity and femininity predicated on the control of women, and eliminating the structural factors that support inequalities are likely to make a significant contribution to preventing intimate partner and sexual violence".[^cite_note-who.int1-453]

According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, "A key strategy in preventing \[domestic violence] is the promotion of respectful, nonviolent relationships through individual, community, and societal level change."[^cite_note-455] Early intervention programs, such as school-based programs to prevent [dating violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dating_violence) are also effective.[^cite_note-who.int1-453] Children who grow up in violent homes may be led to believe that such behavior is a normal part of life, therefore it is important to challenge such attitudes when they are present among these children.[^cite_note-456]

The UN [Sustainable Development Goal 16](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_Development_Goal_16) has a target to end all forms of violence including domestic violence through global advocacy and demand for effective institutions.[^cite_note-457] The joint U.N.-E.U. Spotlight initiative was launched in 2016 to advance this goal worldwide with focus on developing countries and regions.[^cite_note-spotlightCA-458] The Spotlight Initiative is embraced by all implementing partners as critical to economic and political progress in both the implementing and targeted societies.[^cite_note-spotlightCA-458]

## Remembrance

In Canada and Australia, purple benches installed in public places, also known in Canada as "Barb's Benches", honour the memory of victims murdered through domestic and family violence. The first such bench was erected in 2015 to mark the 25th anniversary of the murder of DV victim Barb Ballie by her husband.[^cite_note-459]
